  and render a synthetic one client-side (based on the commanded position, no
  round-trip latency). That's a clean improvement worth adding. I'll incorporate
  it into the implementation alongside the 5 features.

  Now implementing everything. Starting with the protocol layer and working
  outward to both apps.

      233 +/// Everything a renderer needs to make sense of the audio fra
          +mes that follow.                                              
      234 +/// JSON-encoded, sent once at stream start as ``MessageKind/a
          +udioConfig``.                                                 
      235 +public struct AudioStreamConfig: Codable, Sendable, Equatable 
          +{                                                             
      236 +    public let codec: AudioCodec                              
      237 +    public let sampleRate: Double                             
      238 +    public let channelCount: Int                              
      239 +                                                              
      240 +    public init(codec: AudioCodec, sampleRate: Double, channel
          +Count: Int) {                                                 
      241 +        self.codec = codec                                    
      242 +        self.sampleRate = sampleRate                          
      243 +        self.channelCount = channelCount                      
      244 +    }                                                         
      245 +}                                                             
      246 +                                                              
      247 +/// One chunk of PCM audio: a fixed 8-byte header (sampleCount
          + UInt32 + channelCount UInt32)                                
      248 +/// followed by interleaved IEEE 754 Float32 samples, little-e
          +ndian.                                                        
      249 +///                                                           
      250 +/// Binary format because audio arrives at ~100 frames/second 
          +on a 20ms chunk budget and JSON                               
      251 +/// would spend more bytes on punctuation than on the samples.
      252 +public struct EncodedAudioFrame: Sendable, Equatable {        
      253 +    public let sampleCount: Int                               
      254 +    public let channelCount: Int                              
      255 +    /// Interleaved Float32 samples, channel-major order (L0 R
          +0 L1 R1 …).                                                   
      256 +    public let samples: Data                                  
      257 +                                                              
      258 +    public init(sampleCount: Int, channelCount: Int, samples: 
          +Data) {                                                       
      259 +        self.sampleCount = sampleCount                        
      260 +        self.channelCount = channelCount                      
      261 +        self.samples = samples                                
      262 +    }                                                         
      263 +                                                              
      264 +    public static let headerSize = 8                          
      265 +                                                              
      266 +    public func encoded() -> Data {                           
      267 +        var out = Data(capacity: Self.headerSize + samples.cou
          +nt)                                                           
      268 +        out.appendBigEndian(UInt32(clamping: sampleCount))    
      269 +        out.appendBigEndian(UInt32(clamping: channelCount))   
      270 +        out.append(samples)                                   
      271 +        return out                                            
      272 +    }                                                         
      273 +                                                              
      274 +    public init(decoding payload: Data) throws {              
      275 +        guard payload.count >= Self.headerSize else {         
      276 +            throw VideoWireError.truncatedHeader(have: payload
          +.count, need: Self.headerSize)                                
      277 +        }                                                     
      278 +        let base = payload.startIndex                         
      279 +        let sc = payload.readBigEndianUInt32(at: base)        
      280 +        let cc = payload.readBigEndianUInt32(at: base + 4)    
      281 +        let body = Data(payload[(base + Self.headerSize)...]) 
      282 +        self.init(sampleCount: Int(sc), channelCount: Int(cc),
          + samples: body)                                               
      283 +    }                                                         
      284 +}

      ---
      name: redesign-existing-projects
      description: Upgrades existing websites and apps to premium quality. Audits current design, identifies generic AI patterns, and applies high-end design standards without breaking functionality. Works with any CSS framework or vanilla CSS.
      ---
      
      # Redesign Skill
      
      ## How This Works
      
      When applied to an existing project, follow this sequence:
      
      1. **Scan** — Read the codebase. Identify the framework, styling method (Tailwind, vanilla CSS, styled-components, etc.), and current design patterns.
      2. **Diagnose** — Run through the audit below. List every generic pattern, weak point, and missing state you find.
      3. **Fix** — Apply targeted upgrades working with the existing stack. Do not rewrite from scratch. Improve what's there.
      
      ## Design Audit
      
      ### Typography
      
      Check for these problems and fix them:
      
      - **Browser default fonts or Inter everywhere.** Replace with a font that has character. Good options: `Geist`, `Outfit`, `Cabinet Grotesk`, `Satoshi`. For editorial/creative projects, pair a serif header with a sans-serif body.
      - **Headlines lack presence.** Increase size for display text, tighten letter-spacing, reduce line-height. Headlines should feel heavy and intentional.
      - **Body text too wide.** Limit paragraph width to roughly 65 characters. Increase line-height for readability.
      - **Only Regular (400) and Bold (700) weights used.** Introduce Medium (500) and SemiBold (600) for more subtle hierarchy.
      - **Numbers in proportional font.** Use a monospace font or enable tabular figures (`font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums`) for data-heavy interfaces.
      - **Missing letter-spacing adjustments.** Use negative tracking for large headers, positive tracking for small caps or labels.
      - **All-caps subheaders everywhere.** Try lowercase italics, sentence case, or small-caps instead.
      - **Orphaned words.** Single words sitting alone on the last line. Fix with `text-wrap: balance` or `text-wrap: pretty`.
      
      ### Color and Surfaces
      
      - **Pure `#000000` background.** Replace with off-black, dark charcoal, or tinted dark (`#0a0a0a`, `#121212`, or a dark navy).
      - **Oversaturated accent colors.** Keep saturation below 80%. Desaturate accents so they blend with neutrals instead of screaming.
      - **More than one accent color.** Pick one. Remove the rest. Consistency beats variety.
      - **Mixing warm and cool grays.** Stick to one gray family. Tint all grays with a consistent hue (warm or cool, not both).
      - **Purple/blue "AI gradient" aesthetic.** This is the most common AI design fingerprint. Replace with neutral bases and a single, considered accent.
      - **Generic `box-shadow`.** Tint shadows to match the background hue. Use colored shadows (e.g., dark blue shadow on a blue background) instead of pure black at low opacity.
      - **Flat design with zero texture.** Add subtle noise, grain, or micro-patterns to backgrounds. Pure flat vectors feel sterile.
      - **Perfectly even gradients.** Break the uniformity with radial gradients, noise overlays, or mesh gradients instead of standard linear 45-degree fades.
      - **Inconsistent lighting direction.** Audit all shadows to ensure they suggest a single, consistent light source.
      - **Random dark sections in a light mode page (or vice versa).** A single dark-background section breaking an otherwise light page looks like a copy-paste accident. Either commit to a full dark mode or keep a consistent background tone throughout. If contrast is needed, use a slightly darker shade of the same palette — not a sudden jump to `#111` in the middle of a cream page.
      - **Empty, flat sections with no visual depth.** Sections that are just text on a plain background feel unfinished. Add high-quality background imagery (blurred, overlaid, or masked), subtle patterns, or ambient gradients. Use reliable placeholder sources like `https://picsum.photos/seed/{name}/1920/1080` when real assets are not available. Experiment with background images behind hero sections, feature blocks, or CTAs — even a subtle full-width photo at low opacity adds presence.
      
      ### Layout
      
      - **Everything centered and symmetrical.** Break symmetry with offset margins, mixed aspect ratios, or left-aligned headers over centered content.
      - **Three equal card columns as feature row.** This is the most generic AI layout. Replace with a 2-column zig-zag, asymmetric grid, horizontal scroll, or masonry layout.
      - **Using `height: 100vh` for full-screen sections.** Replace with `min-height: 100dvh` to prevent layout jumping on mobile browsers (iOS Safari viewport bug).
      - **Complex flexbox percentage math.** Replace with CSS Grid for reliable multi-column structures.
      - **No max-width container.** Add a container constraint (around 1200-1440px) with auto margins so content doesn't stretch edge-to-edge on wide screens.
      - **Cards of equal height forced by flexbox.** Allow variable heights or use masonry when content varies in length.
      - **Uniform border-radius on everything.** Vary the radius: tighter on inner elements, softer on containers.
      - **No overlap or depth.** Elements sit flat next to each other. Use negative margins to create layering and visual depth.
      - **Symmetrical vertical padding.** Top and bottom padding are always identical. Adjust optically — bottom padding often needs to be slightly larger.
      - **Dashboard always has a left sidebar.** Try top navigation, a floating command menu, or a collapsible panel instead.
      - **Missing whitespace.** Double the spacing. Let the design breathe. Dense layouts work for data dashboards, not for marketing pages.
      - **Buttons not bottom-aligned in card groups.** When cards have different content lengths, CTAs end up at random heights. Pin buttons to the bottom of each card so they form a clean horizontal line regardless of content above.
      - **Feature lists starting at different vertical positions.** In pricing tables or comparison cards, the list of features should start at the same Y position across all columns. Use consistent spacing above the list or fixed-height title/price blocks.
      - **Inconsistent vertical rhythm in side-by-side elements.** When placing cards, columns, or panels next to each other, align shared elements (titles, descriptions, prices, buttons) across all items. Misaligned baselines make the layout look broken.
      - **Mathematical alignment that looks optically wrong.** Centering by the math doesn't always look centered to the eye. Icons next to text, play buttons in circles, or text in buttons often need 1-2px optical adjustments to feel right.
      
      ### Interactivity and States
      
      - **No hover states on buttons.** Add background shift, slight scale, or translate on hover.
      - **No active/pressed feedback.** Add a subtle `scale(0.98)` or `translateY(1px)` on press to simulate a physical click.
      - **Instant transitions with zero duration.** Add smooth transitions (200-300ms) to all interactive elements.
      - **Missing focus ring.** Ensure visible focus indicators for keyboard navigation. This is an accessibility requirement, not optional.
      - **No loading states.** Replace generic circular spinners with skeleton loaders that match the layout shape.
      - **No empty states.** An empty dashboard showing nothing is a missed opportunity. Design a composed "getting started" view.
      - **No error states.** Add clear, inline error messages for forms. Do not use `window.alert()`.
      - **Dead links.** Buttons that link to `#`. Either link to real destinations or visually disable them.
      - **No indication of current page in navigation.** Style the active nav link differently so users know where they are.
      - **Scroll jumping.** Anchor clicks jump instantly. Add `scroll-behavior: smooth`.
      - **Animations using `top`, `left`, `width`, `height`.** Switch to `transform` and `opacity` for GPU-accelerated, smooth animation.
      
      ### Content
      
      - **Generic names like "John Doe" or "Jane Smith".** Use diverse, realistic-sounding names.
      - **Fake round numbers like `99.99%`, `50%`, `$100.00`.** Use organic, messy data: `47.2%`, `$99.00`, `+1 (312) 847-1928`.
      - **Placeholder company names like "Acme Corp", "Nexus", "SmartFlow".** Invent contextual, believable brand names.
      - **AI copywriting cliches.** Never use "Elevate", "Seamless", "Unleash", "Next-Gen", "Game-changer", "Delve", "Tapestry", or "In the world of...". Write plain, specific language.
      - **Exclamation marks in success messages.** Remove them. Be confident, not loud.
      - **"Oops!" error messages.** Be direct: "Connection failed. Please try again."
      - **Passive voice.** Use active voice: "We couldn't save your changes" instead of "Mistakes were made."
      - **All blog post dates identical.** Randomize dates to appear real.
      - **Same avatar image for multiple users.** Use unique assets for every distinct person.
      - **Lorem Ipsum.** Never use placeholder latin text. Write real draft copy.
      - **Title Case On Every Header.** Use sentence case instead.
      
      ### Component Patterns
      
      - **Generic card look (border + shadow + white background).** Remove the border, or use only background color, or use only spacing. Cards should exist only when elevation communicates hierarchy.
      - **Always one filled button + one ghost button.** Add text links or tertiary styles to reduce visual noise.
      - **Pill-shaped "New" and "Beta" badges.** Try square badges, flags, or plain text labels.
      - **Accordion FAQ sections.** Use a side-by-side list, searchable help, or inline progressive disclosure.
      - **3-card carousel testimonials with dots.** Replace with a masonry wall, embedded social posts, or a single rotating quote.
      - **Pricing table with 3 towers.** Highlight the recommended tier with color and emphasis, not just extra height.
      - **Modals for everything.** Use inline editing, slide-over panels, or expandable sections instead of popups for simple actions.
      - **Avatar circles exclusively.** Try squircles or rounded squares for a less generic look.
      - **Light/dark toggle always a sun/moon switch.** Use a dropdown, system preference detection, or integrate it into settings.
      - **Footer link farm with 4 columns.** Simplify. Focus on main navigational paths and legally required links.
      
      ### Iconography
      
      - **Lucide or Feather icons exclusively.** These are the "default" AI icon choice. Use Phosphor, Heroicons, or a custom set for differentiation.
      - **Rocketship for "Launch", shield for "Security".** Replace cliche metaphors with less obvious icons (bolt, fingerprint, spark, vault).
      - **Inconsistent stroke widths across icons.** Audit all icons and standardize to one stroke weight.
      - **Missing favicon.** Always include a branded favicon.
      - **Stock "diverse team" photos.** Use real team photos, candid shots, or a consistent illustration style instead of uncanny stock imagery.
      
      ### Code Quality
      
      - **Div soup.** Use semantic HTML: `<nav>`, `<main>`, `<article>`, `<aside>`, `<section>`.
      - **Inline styles mixed with CSS classes.** Move all styling to the project's styling system.
      - **Hardcoded pixel widths.** Use relative units (`%`, `rem`, `em`, `max-width`) for flexible layouts.
      - **Missing alt text on images.** Describe image content for screen readers. Never leave `alt=""` or `alt="image"` on meaningful images.
      - **Arbitrary z-index values like `9999`.** Establish a clean z-index scale in the theme/variables.
      - **Commented-out dead code.** Remove all debug artifacts before shipping.
      - **Import hallucinations.** Check that every import actually exists in `package.json` or the project dependencies.
      - **Missing meta tags.** Add proper `<title>`, `description`, `og:image`, and social sharing meta tags.
      
      ### Strategic Omissions (What AI Typically Forgets)
      
      - **No legal links.** Add privacy policy and terms of service links in the footer.
      - **No "back" navigation.** Dead ends in user flows. Every page needs a way back.
      - **No custom 404 page.** Design a helpful, branded "page not found" experience.
      - **No form validation.** Add client-side validation for emails, required fields, and format checks.
      - **No "skip to content" link.** Essential for keyboard users. Add a hidden skip-link.
      - **No cookie consent.** If required by jurisdiction, add a compliant consent banner.
      
      ## Upgrade Techniques
      
      When upgrading a project, pull from these high-impact techniques to replace generic patterns:
      
      ### Typography Upgrades
      - **Variable font animation.** Interpolate weight or width on scroll or hover for text that feels alive.
      - **Outlined-to-fill transitions.** Text starts as a stroke outline and fills with color on scroll entry or interaction.
      - **Text mask reveals.** Large typography acting as a window to video or animated imagery behind it.
      
      ### Layout Upgrades
      - **Broken grid / asymmetry.** Elements that deliberately ignore column structure — overlapping, bleeding off-screen, or offset with calculated randomness.
      - **Whitespace maximization.** Aggressive use of negative space to force focus on a single element.
      - **Parallax card stacks.** Sections that stick and physically stack over each other during scroll.
      - **Split-screen scroll.** Two halves of the screen sliding in opposite directions.
      
      ### Motion Upgrades
      - **Smooth scroll with inertia.** Decouple scrolling from browser defaults for a heavier, cinematic feel.
      - **Staggered entry.** Elements cascade in with slight delays, combining Y-axis translation with opacity fade. Never mount everything at once.
      - **Spring physics.** Replace linear easing with spring-based motion for a natural, weighty feel on all interactive elements.
      - **Scroll-driven reveals.** Content entering through expanding masks, wipes, or draw-on SVG paths tied to scroll progress.
      
      ### Surface Upgrades
      - **True glassmorphism.** Go beyond `backdrop-filter: blur`. Add a 1px inner border and a subtle inner shadow to simulate edge refraction.
      - **Spotlight borders.** Card borders that illuminate dynamically under the cursor.
      - **Grain and noise overlays.** A fixed, pointer-events-none overlay with subtle noise to break digital flatness.
      - **Colored, tinted shadows.** Shadows that carry the hue of the background rather than using generic black.
      
      ## Fix Priority
      
      Apply changes in this order for maximum visual impact with minimum risk:
      
      1. **Font swap** — biggest instant improvement, lowest risk
      2. **Color palette cleanup** — remove clashing or oversaturated colors
      3. **Hover and active states** — makes the interface feel alive
      4. **Layout and spacing** — proper grid, max-width, consistent padding
      5. **Replace generic components** — swap cliche patterns for modern alternatives
      6. **Add loading, empty, and error states** — makes it feel finished
      7. **Polish typography scale and spacing** — the premium final touch
      
      ## Rules
      
      - Work with the existing tech stack. Do not migrate frameworks or styling libraries.
      - Do not break existing functionality. Test after every change.
      - Before importing any new library, check the project's dependency file first.
      - If the project uses Tailwind, check the version (v3 vs v4) before modifying config.
      - If the project has no framework, use vanilla CSS.
      - Keep changes reviewable and focused. Small, targeted improvements over big rewrites.
      ---
      name: diagnosing-bugs
      description: Diagnosis loop for hard bugs and performance regressions. Use when the user says "diagnose"/"debug this", or reports something broken/throwing/failing/slow.
      ---
      
      # Diagnosing Bugs
      
      A discipline for hard bugs. Skip phases only when explicitly justified.
      
      When exploring the codebase, read `CONTEXT.md` (if it exists) to get a clear mental model of the relevant modules, and check ADRs in the area you're touching.
      
      ## Redact
      
      This skill has you show commands, outputs and captured artifacts. **Redact every secret first**: write `<REDACTED>` in its place. Build loops against env vars, so the credential stays in the environment rather than in what you show. Captured artifacts carry auth headers: quote only the lines that carry the signal.
      
      If the redacted output is not enough to diagnose the bug, say so and ask the user.
      
      ## Phase 1: Build a feedback loop
      
      **This is the skill.** Everything else is mechanical. If you have a **tight** pass/fail signal for the bug (one that goes red on _this_ bug), you will find the cause; bisection, hypothesis-testing, and instrumentation all just consume it. If you don't have one, no amount of staring at code will save you.
      
      Spend disproportionate effort here. **Be aggressive. Be creative. Refuse to give up.**
      
      ### Ways to construct one, in roughly this order
      
      1. **Failing test** at whatever seam reaches the bug: unit, integration, e2e.
      2. **Curl / HTTP script** against a running dev server.
      3. **CLI invocation** with a fixture input, diffing stdout against a known-good snapshot.
      4. **Headless browser script** (Playwright / Puppeteer) that drives the UI and asserts on DOM/console/network.
      5. **Replay a captured trace.** Save a real network request / payload / event log to disk; replay it through the code path in isolation.
      6. **Throwaway harness.** Spin up a minimal subset of the system (one service, mocked deps) that exercises the bug code path with a single function call.
      7. **Property / fuzz loop.** If the bug is "sometimes wrong output", run 1000 random inputs and look for the failure mode.
      8. **Bisection harness.** If the bug appeared between two known states (commit, dataset, version), automate "boot at state X, check, repeat" so you can `git bisect run` it.
      9. **Differential loop.** Run the same input through old-version vs new-version (or two configs) and diff outputs.
      10. **HITL bash script.** Last resort. If a human must click, drive _them_ with `scripts/hitl-loop.template.sh` so the loop is still structured. Captured output feeds back to you.
      
      Build the right feedback loop, and the bug is 90% fixed.
      
      ### Tighten the loop
      
      Treat the loop as a product. Once you have _a_ loop, **tighten** it:
      
      - Can I make it faster? (Cache setup, skip unrelated init, narrow the test scope.)
      - Can I make the signal sharper? (Assert on the specific symptom, not "didn't crash".)
      - Can I make it more deterministic? (Pin time, seed RNG, isolate filesystem, freeze network.)
      
      A 30-second flaky loop is barely better than no loop; a 2-second deterministic one is tight, a debugging superpower.
      
      ### Non-deterministic bugs
      
      The goal is not a clean repro but a **higher reproduction rate**. Loop the trigger 100×, parallelise, add stress, narrow timing windows, inject sleeps. A 50%-flake bug is debuggable; 1% is not, so keep raising the rate until it's debuggable.
      
      ### When you genuinely cannot build a loop
      
      Stop and say so explicitly. List what you tried. Ask the user for: (a) access to whatever environment reproduces it, (b) a redacted captured artifact (HAR file, log dump, core dump, screen recording with timestamps), or (c) permission to add temporary production instrumentation. Do **not** proceed to hypothesise without a loop.
      
      ### Completion criterion: a tight loop that goes red
      
      Phase 1 is done when the loop is **tight** and **red-capable**: you can name **one command** (a script path, a test invocation, a curl) that you have **already run at least once** (show the invocation and its output, redacted), and that is:
      
      - [ ] **Red-capable**: it drives the actual bug code path and asserts the **user's exact symptom**, so it can go red on this bug and green once fixed. Not "runs without erroring"; it must be able to _catch this specific bug_.
      - [ ] **Deterministic**: same verdict every run (flaky bugs: a pinned, high reproduction rate, per above).
      - [ ] **Fast**: seconds, not minutes.
      - [ ] **Agent-runnable**: you can run it unattended; a human in the loop only via `scripts/hitl-loop.template.sh`.
      
      If you catch yourself reading code to build a theory before this command exists, **stop: jumping straight to a hypothesis is the exact failure this skill prevents.** No red-capable command, no Phase 2.
      
      ## Phase 2: Reproduce + minimise
      
      Run the loop. Watch it go red as the bug appears.
      
      Confirm:
      
      - [ ] The loop produces the failure mode the **user** described, not a different failure that happens to be nearby. Wrong bug = wrong fix.
      - [ ] The failure is reproducible across multiple runs (or, for non-deterministic bugs, reproducible at a high enough rate to debug against).
      - [ ] You have captured the exact symptom (error message, wrong output, slow timing) so later phases can verify the fix actually addresses it.
      
      ### Minimise
      
      Once it's red, shrink the repro to the **smallest scenario that still goes red**. Cut inputs, callers, config, data, and steps **one at a time**, re-running the loop after each cut, and keep only what's load-bearing for the failure.
      
      Why bother: a minimal repro shrinks the hypothesis space in Phase 3 (fewer moving parts left to suspect) and becomes the clean regression test in Phase 5.
      
      Done when **every remaining element is load-bearing**: removing any one of them makes the loop go green.
      
      Do not proceed until you have reproduced **and** minimised.
      
      ## Phase 3: Hypothesise
      
      Generate **3–5 ranked hypotheses** before testing any of them. Single-hypothesis generation anchors on the first plausible idea.
      
      Each hypothesis must be **falsifiable**: state the prediction it makes.
      
      > Format: "If <X> is the cause, then <changing Y> will make the bug disappear / <changing Z> will make it worse."
      
      If you cannot state the prediction, the hypothesis is a vibe: discard or sharpen it.
      
      **Show the ranked list to the user before testing.** They often have domain knowledge that re-ranks instantly ("we just deployed a change to #3"), or know hypotheses they've already ruled out. Cheap checkpoint, big time saver. Don't block on it; proceed with your ranking if the user is AFK.
      
      ## Phase 4: Instrument
      
      Each probe must map to a specific prediction from Phase 3. **Change one variable at a time.**
      
      Tool preference:
      
      1. **Debugger / REPL inspection** if the env supports it. One breakpoint beats ten logs.
      2. **Targeted logs** at the boundaries that distinguish hypotheses.
      3. Never "log everything and grep".
      
      **Tag every debug log** with a unique prefix, e.g. `[DEBUG-a4f2]`. Cleanup at the end becomes a single grep. Untagged logs survive; tagged logs die.
      
      **Perf branch.** For performance regressions, logs are usually wrong. Instead: establish a baseline measurement (timing harness, `performance.now()`, profiler, query plan), then bisect. Measure first, fix second.
      
      ## Phase 5: Fix + regression test
      
      Write the regression test **before the fix**, but only if there is a **correct seam** for it.
      
      A correct seam is one where the test exercises the **real bug pattern** as it occurs at the call site. If the only available seam is too shallow (single-caller test when the bug needs multiple callers, unit test that can't replicate the chain that triggered the bug), a regression test there gives false confidence.
      
      **If no correct seam exists, that itself is the finding.** Note it. The codebase architecture is preventing the bug from being locked down. Flag this for the next phase.
      
      If a correct seam exists:
      
      1. Turn the minimised repro into a failing test at that seam.
      2. Watch it fail.
      3. Apply the fix.
      4. Watch it pass.
      5. Re-run the Phase 1 feedback loop against the original (un-minimised) scenario.
      
      ## Phase 6: Cleanup
      
      Required before declaring done:
      
      - [ ] Original repro no longer reproduces (re-run the Phase 1 loop)
      - [ ] Regression test passes (or absence of seam is documented)
      - [ ] All `[DEBUG-...]` instrumentation removed (`grep` the prefix)
      - [ ] Throwaway prototypes deleted (or moved to a clearly-marked debug location)
      - [ ] The hypothesis that turned out correct is stated in the commit / PR message, so the next debugger learns

      # MacCast domain glossary
      
      ## Capture source
      
      Something the host can stream now: a display, a running application, or a window.
      
      ## Running application
      
      A user-facing application process that currently owns capturable content. It has a process
      identity and can therefore be selected as a capture source.
      
      ## Installed application
      
      A user-facing application available on the host but not currently running. It can be launched,
      but it is not a capture source until it becomes a running application.
      
      ## Application catalogue
      
      The host's current, user-facing list of running and installed applications. Background agents,
      menu extras, helper processes, and duplicate instances are not applications in this catalogue.
      # MacCast — agent working notes
      
      Everything a fresh agent needs that is **not** derivable from reading the source. Written
      2026-09-14 by the agent that built the bulk of the system. If you are picking this up cold,
      read this top to bottom before touching anything — several of the traps below cost hours.
      
      ---
      
      ## 1. What this is
      
      A cross-platform remote desktop system:
      
      - **`MacCastHost`** — a macOS app that captures the screen, encodes it, and injects synthetic
        input. Non-sandboxed on purpose (a sandboxed app cannot post `CGEvent`s into other apps,
        which is the entire function of the app — so the Mac App Store is not a target).
      - **`MacCast`** — an iOS app that renders the stream and sends gestures/keystrokes back.
      - **`RemoteCore`** — a local Swift package with everything shared: framing, protocol,
        crypto, coordinate mapping.
      
      Communication is over local Wi-Fi. The design goal that drives most decisions: **the Mac is
      recognised across networks with no user interaction after the first pairing.**
      
      ### Targets
      
      `xcodebuild -list -project MacCast.xcodeproj`:
      
      ```
      Targets: MacCast (iOS), MacCastTests, MacCastUITests, MacCastHost (macOS)
      Schemes: MacCast, MacCastHost, RemoteCore
      Packages: swift-certificates 1.20.0, swift-crypto 4.5.2, swift-asn1 1.7.2, RemoteCore (local)
      ```
      
      The project uses `PBXFileSystemSynchronizedRootGroup` (objectVersion 77), so **new `.swift`
      files under a synced folder need zero pbxproj edits.** Do not hand-write file entries into
      build phases.
      
      ---
      
      ## 2. Commands that actually work
      
      ```bash
      # Build the iOS client for the physical device
      xcodebuild -project MacCast.xcodeproj -scheme MacCast \
        -destination 'id=8A51FAD5-A897-57CD-9C09-395D9F2C258A' build
      
      # Build/run the host
      xcodebuild -project MacCast.xcodeproj -scheme MacCastHost -configuration Debug build
      open ~/Library/Developer/Xcode/DerivedData/MacCast-*/Build/Products/Debug/Peering.app
      
      # RemoteCore unit tests (100 tests, 14 suites, all green as of 2026-09-14)
      cd RemoteCore && swift test
      
      # Install + launch on the connected iPhone
      xcrun devicectl device install app --device 8A51FAD5-A897-57CD-9C09-395D9F2C258A \
        ~/Library/Developer/Xcode/DerivedData/MacCast-*/Build/Products/Debug-iphoneos/MacCast.app
      xcrun devicectl device process launch --device 8A51FAD5-A897-57CD-9C09-395D9F2C258A \
        pl.dmq.peering
      
      # Read the host log  (NOTE: /usr/bin/log explicitly — see trap #1)
      /usr/bin/log show --last 5m --style compact \
        --predicate 'subsystem == "pl.dmq.peering.host"'
      ```
      
      **Always build with `-scheme`, never `-target`.** With `-target` the build settings that make
      the targets work (notably `INFOPLIST_KEY_*`) are not applied.
      
      Log subsystems are `pl.dmq.peering.host` (the Mac) and `pl.dmq.peering` (the phone), categories
      `session`, `video`, `capture`, `encoder`.
      
      ---
      
      ## 3. Verification tooling
      
      This project has a script client that can prove almost everything without the iOS UI. **Use
      it.** Phases 1–5 of the plan were all verified with it before any iOS UI existed.
      
      ### `Tools/run-probe.sh`
      
      Builds, **signs**, and runs `Tools/MacCastProbe`. The signing step is load-bearing, not
      housekeeping — read the comment at the top of the script before you change it.
      
      ```bash
      Tools/run-probe.sh --drive --accept --id probe-client-2
      Tools/run-probe.sh --hold-drop 55 --accept --id probe-client-2
      Tools/run-probe.sh --video-recv --video-recv-seconds 5 --accept --id probe-client-2
      Tools/run-probe.sh --browse                    # Bonjour discovery only
      Tools/run-probe.sh --reset --id probe-client-3 --accept   # force a fresh identity + pairing
      ```
      
      Full flag list: `Tools/run-probe.sh --help`. Highlights: `--drive` (moves the real cursor and
      verifies it lands), `--type` / `--key` / `--combo` / `--space` (injection), `--flags LABEL`
      (prints held modifiers — use it to prove nothing is stuck), `--video` / `--video-only` /
      `--video-recv`, `--switch-to src@seconds`, `--catalog`, `--find`.
      
      ### `Tools/activity.sh [seconds]`
      
      Drives the cursor in a Lissajous path (incommensurate axes, so the picture never settles into
      a loop the encoder compresses away) and restores it on exit. **Any test of frame ordering,
      frame rate, or queue depth needs the screen moving** — see trap #7. Run it in the background
      across the measurement:
      
      ```bash
      Tools/activity.sh 20 & sleep 2; Tools/run-probe.sh --video-recv --accept --id probe-client-2
      ```
      
      ### The regression sweep
      
      The full sweep that was green at the end of the build:
      
      ```bash
      ID="--accept --id probe-client-2"     # NOTE: see trap #3 about shell quoting
      Tools/run-probe.sh $ID --drive
      Tools/run-probe.sh $ID --hold-drop 55
      Tools/run-probe.sh $ID --hold-button-drop 0
      Tools/run-probe.sh $ID --type "hello" --combo cmd:0 --space right --flags AFTER
      Tools/run-probe.sh $ID --catalog
      Tools/run-probe.sh $ID --video-recv --video-recv-seconds 5
      Tools/run-probe.sh $ID --video-only          # expects refusal
      Tools/run-probe.sh $ID --switch-to window:NNNN@1 --switch-to application:NNNN@2 ...
      ```
      
      ---
      
      ## 4. Architecture
      
      ### Wire protocol (`RemoteCore`)
      
      **Framing — built first, because every command depends on it:**
      
      ```
      ┌──────────────┬─────────┬─────────────────────┐
      │ u32 length   │ u8 kind │ payload (length-1)  │
      │ big-endian   │         │                     │
      └──────────────┴─────────┴─────────────────────┘
      ```
      
      `FrameDecoder` accumulates bytes and emits whole frames. Hard 16 MiB length cap enforced
      **before allocating** — otherwise a corrupt length field is an instant memory-exhaustion DoS.
      
      **Two TCP connections, not one** — control and video. Sharing one stream causes head-of-line
      blocking: a 200 KB keyframe sits in front of the next mouse move. Control latency is what a
      user feels. One `NWListener` on **port 51515** accepts both; the first frame declares the role.
      
      **Handshake:** `clientHello` → `serverHello` → TLS → SAS pairing (unpaired) or pin check
      (paired). Then sealed.
      
      ### Security design (all of this is deliberate; don't "simplify" it)
      
      - **TLS 1.3 with a runtime-generated self-signed identity**, minted on the host and pinned by
        the client. Self-signed is not weaker than CA-signed *provided the client pins* — if
        anything stronger, since there is no CA to coerce.
      - **TLS-PSK was considered and rejected**, and the reason is worth keeping: the only workable
        PSK suite is TLS 1.2-only and is plain PSK with **no (EC)DHE**, so no forward secrecy. A
        passive eavesdropper recording one handshake could brute-force a 6-digit PIN offline (10⁶
        HMACs is instant). That disqualifies it for a PIN-based scheme.
      - **Pairing is SAS / numeric comparison** (the Bluetooth LE model), *not* a typed PIN. A typed
        6-digit PIN used as a client→host proof is offline-brute-forceable by an active attacker
        during first pairing: the attacker substitutes their own identity, receives the PIN-derived
        MAC over *their* transcript, and grinds all 10⁶ candidates offline. With SAS the two screens
        show 6 digits derived from the handshake transcript and the user confirms they match — an
        active MITM now succeeds with probability 10⁻⁶ per attempt and cannot grind offline.
      - After pairing the host's long-term identity key is pinned in the **iOS Keychain** with
        `kSecAttrAccessibleAfterFirstUnlockThisDeviceOnly`; later connections need no interaction.
      - Post-pairing client→host auth is **mTLS** (`sec_protocol_options_set_challenge_block`), not
        an app-layer bearer token.
      - Client pins the server's **SPKI**, not leaf bytes, so the cert can rotate without re-pairing.
      - Transcript hashing must be in a **fixed agreed order** and cover every exchanged field, or
        the comparison is meaningless. Comparisons are **constant-time** (Swift's `Data ==`
        short-circuits). Both are pinned by unit tests.
      
      **The SAS pairing is automatable from the client side** — `handlePairConfirm` on the client
      acts on the *client's* `pairConfirm` frame; the host merely displays its code. That is why the
      probe's `--accept` works, and it is not a hole (the client is the one being asked to confirm).
      
      ### Capture sources
      
      ```swift
      public enum CaptureSource: Codable, Sendable, Hashable {
          case display(UInt32)       // CGDirectDisplayID raw value
          case application(Int32)    // pid_t — all that app's windows, composited
          case window(UInt32)        // CGWindowID raw value
      }
      ```
      
      Platform-neutral raw values **on purpose** — `CGDirectDisplayID`/`CGWindowID` live in
      macOS-only headers; using them directly breaks the iOS build. Same reason `RemoteCore` speaks
      `UInt16` keycodes and `UInt` modifiers rather than `CGKeyCode`/`NSEvent.ModifierFlags`.
      
      One `CaptureSourceResolver` owns the translation to `SCContentFilter`, so the rest of the
      pipeline never switches on source kind. The catalogue is **live** — re-enumerated on
      `NSWorkspace` launch/terminate and `didChangeScreenParametersNotification`.
      
      ### Streaming pipeline
      
      - **Capture (host):** `SCShareableContent` → `SCDisplay` by `CGDirectDisplayID` →
        `SCContentFilter(display:excludingWindows:)`. `queueDepth` **2–3** (default 8 is the primary
        latency knob). `captureResolution = .nominal`. `pixelFormat = '420v'` (the encoder's native
        input, skips a colour conversion). Do **not** use `scalesToFit`.
      - **Encode:** `VTCompressionSessionEncodeFrameWithOutputHandler` — the only variant whose
        callback is `CM_SWIFT_SENDABLE`, which is the crux of making it work under Swift 6 strict
        concurrency. `AllowFrameReordering` **defaults to `true` and must be set `false`** (B-frames
        add latency). Plus `EnableLowLatencyRateControl`, `RealTime`, `ExpectedFrameRate`,
        `PrioritizeEncodingSpeedOverQuality`. H.264 to start; codec is behind an enum.
      - **Parameter sets** live in the format description attached to the output sample buffer, not
        in the sample data. Keep **AVCC** (4-byte length-prefixed NALUs) — that is what VideoToolbox
        emits and what the client needs.
      - **Decode/render (client):** `AVSampleBufferDisplayLayer.sampleBufferRenderer`. No separate
        `VTDecompressionSession` — the renderer decodes in hardware internally. Build `CMSampleBuffer`s
        with `CMSampleBufferCreateReady` (`decodeTimeStamp = .invalid`, valid because reordering is
        off). Use `renderer.enqueue/flush/isReadyForMoreMediaData` (the *renderer's*, not the layer's).
      - **Force a keyframe the moment a client connects** — never by tearing down the session.
      
      Reference for the directory layout: `RemoteCore/Sources/RemoteCore/` holds `Framing`,
      `Handshake`, `PairingSAS`, `TLSConfig`, `SelfSignedIdentity`, `PeerPinning`, `DeviceIdentity`,
      `ClientSession`, `ClientTransport`, `RemoteCommand`, `InputTransform`, `InputEncoding`,
      `CaptureSource`, `VideoWire`, `ServiceDiscovery`, `ConstantTime`, `ProtocolInfo`.

      import CoreGraphics
      import CoreMedia
      import CoreVideo
      import Foundation
      import RemoteCore
      import os
      
      private let overlayLog = Logger(subsystem: "pl.dmq.peering.host", category: "capture")
      
      /// A larger pointer, drawn into the picture because the system will not draw one.
      ///
      /// ScreenCaptureKit's only controls over the cursor are switches: `showsCursor` shows or hides
      /// the system's own pointer, `showMouseClicks` draws a ring around a click, and neither has a
      /// size beside it. So the pointer a frame carries is always the one on the Mac, about sixteen
      /// points tall, and that picture is the whole display — 1512×982 points on a built-in panel,
      /// which the client draws in roughly 390. A factor of four puts the pointer at something like
      /// four points on a phone, which is the complaint this answers, and the only way to answer it
      /// is to have the stream leave the cursor out and put a bigger one back.
      ///
      /// **The luma plane and nothing else.** Frames arrive as `420v`, bi-planar video-range YCbCr,
      /// and a pointer has no colour in it: the system's own is white with a black edge, and two
      /// shades are all that takes. Writing Y alone is one pass over a few hundred rows, with no
      /// colour conversion, no second pixel format, and no work on the chroma plane at all. The two
      /// values are 16 and 235 rather than 0 and 255, which matters more than it looks: this is
      /// video range, where 0 and 255 lie outside what the encoder is told to expect, and
      /// out-of-range luma is not clamped on the way through — it comes back as a crushed edge or a
      /// ring around the pointer.
      ///
      /// **Aliased on purpose.** Every edge is a hard step between those two values. At several
      /// times the size of the system's pointer the steps stop being visible, and the alternative is
      /// a coverage calculation per pixel on a path that runs sixty times a second.
      ///
      /// **Nothing here allocates per frame.** The shape is a polygon in the pointer's own units —
      /// tip at the origin, one unit tall — built once at construction, and a frame's size only ever
      /// multiplies it. The rasteriser's scratch is a fixed array it reuses. The one object a frame
      /// does produce is the `CGEvent` the pointer's position is read from, which is the only way
      /// the window server is asked where the cursor is.
      ///
      /// The system pointer deliberately remains enabled in ScreenCaptureKit underneath this one.
      /// Its movement makes the stream produce real consecutive frames even on a quiet desktop;
      /// this larger opaque shape covers it at the same tip in the frame sent to the phone.
      ///
      /// The synthetic pipeline cannot use this and does not try: generated frames describe no
      /// screen, so there is no coordinate space for a pointer to be placed in. See
      /// ``CapturePipeline/ingest(_:)``.
      final class CursorOverlay: @unchecked Sendable {
          /// How much larger than the system's own pointer the drawn one is.
          ///
          /// A size in points would be no use: the client scales the picture to its own screen, and
          /// a point is not a fixed share of a picture whose size follows the display it came from.
          /// A fraction of the frame's height is, and it survives a source switch as well — the
          /// pointer keeps its share of the picture however large the picture is.
          enum Size: String, CaseIterable, Sendable {
              case small
              case medium
              case large
      
              /// The pointer's height, as a fraction of the frame's height.
              ///
              /// The system's arrow is about sixteen points on a display around a thousand points
              /// tall, so it takes up a little over one and a half percent of the height. Each case
              /// here is a multiple of that, which is the honest way to describe them: this pointer
              /// is two to four times the size of the one the Mac is showing.
              var fractionOfHeight: CGFloat {
                  switch self {
                  case .small: return 0.032   // roughly twice the system's pointer
                  case .medium: return 0.048  // roughly three times
                  case .large: return 0.064   // roughly four times
                  }
              }
          }
      
          /// The two values a pointer is drawn with, in video range.
          private static let ink: UInt8 = 235
          private static let edge: UInt8 = 16
      
          /// The drawn pointer's edge, as a fraction of its own height.
          ///
          /// Half of it is stroked outside the outline and half of it ends up under the fill, so the
          /// edge that shows is thinner than this number. It scales with the pointer rather than
          /// being a fixed number of pixels, because what an edge is for is separating the pointer
          /// from whatever is behind it, and that is the one thing that fails if the edge is a
          /// hairline under a pointer drawn four times the size of the system's.
          private static let edgeWidth: CGFloat = 0.09
      
          /// The pointer's outline: tip at the origin, y down, in units of its own height.
          ///
          /// A polygon rather than a bitmap of the system's cursor, because a bitmap is what cannot
          /// be made bigger: the cursor art is sixteen pixels with one-pixel steps in its edges, and
          /// scaling it up is a pointer four times the size with the same number of steps, which
          /// looks like a staircase. These are the classic proportions — the vertical left edge, the
          /// notch where the tail leaves the head, and the tail's foot.
          private static let outline: [CGPoint] = [
              CGPoint(x: 0.000, y: 0.000),  // the tip, which is where the pointer is
              CGPoint(x: 0.000, y: 0.707),  // down the left edge, which is vertical
              CGPoint(x: 0.201, y: 0.512),  // up into the notch between the head and the tail
              CGPoint(x: 0.390, y: 0.963),  // down the tail's left side to its foot
              CGPoint(x: 0.549, y: 0.896),  // across the foot
              CGPoint(x: 0.360, y: 0.445),  // back up the tail's right side
              CGPoint(x: 0.622, y: 0.445),  // out to the head's bottom right corner
          ]
      
          /// One polygon to fill, and what to fill it with.
          private struct Pass {
              let range: Range<Int>
              let value: UInt8
          }
      
          private let lock = NSLock()
      
          /// The polygons, in the pointer's own units, each closed by repeating its first point so
          /// that the rasteriser never has to wrap an index around the end of a range.
          ///
          /// The edge quads come first and the outline last, which is the whole of the drawing
          /// order: the fill goes over the inner half of its own edge and leaves the outer half
          /// showing.
          private let vertices: [CGPoint]
          private let passes: [Pass]
      
          /// Where a scanline's crossings are collected. A pointer's polygon crosses a row at most
          /// seven times, so this is never close to full — it is a fixed array rather than one per
          /// call because a frame draws eight polygons and this runs at sixty frames a second.
          private var intersections = [CGFloat](repeating: 0, count: 16)
      
          /// Whether a refusal has been logged yet. See ``report(_:)``.
          private var reportedRefusal = false
      
          /// Where the pointer's tip was last drawn into a frame, to the nearest pixel of the
          /// picture.
          ///
          /// Nil whenever no frame carries a pointer: the overlay is off, or the pointer has been
          /// somewhere other than over the captured content since the last frame. An idle sample is
          /// only worth a frame of its own when the tip is not here now — the client is already

      import RemoteCore
      import SwiftUI
      import UIKit
      
      /// What the fingers on the picture are for.
      ///
      /// A choice the user makes and can see, not a heuristic. It used to be a hidden flag that
      /// pinch-zoom flipped on its own, so the same two-finger drag either scrolled the Mac or moved
      /// the view depending on something the user had done earlier in a different gesture — which is
      /// impossible to build a habit around.
      enum InteractionMode: String, CaseIterable, Identifiable {
          /// Fingers drive the Mac: tap and drag are the pointer, two fingers scroll it.
          case control
          /// Fingers drive the picture: drag pans, pinch zooms, and nothing reaches the Mac.
          case navigate
          /// The picture can be inspected, while every input command is blocked.
          case observe
          /// Trackpad mode: the whole screen is treated as a trackpad surface. One finger moves
          /// the pointer by relative delta (no absolute mapping), two fingers scroll, tap to click.
          case trackpad
      
          var id: String { rawValue }
      
          var label: String {
              switch self {
              case .control: return "View & scroll"
              case .navigate: return "Drag & zoom"
              case .observe: return "Observe"
              case .trackpad: return "Trackpad"
              }
          }
      
          var symbol: String {
              switch self {
              case .control: return "hand.point.up.left"
              case .navigate: return "arrow.up.left.and.arrow.down.right"
              case .observe: return "eye"
              case .trackpad: return "rectangle.and.hand.point.up.left.fill"
              }
          }
      
          var toggled: InteractionMode {
              switch self {
              case .control: return .navigate
              case .navigate: return .trackpad
              case .trackpad: return .observe
              case .observe: return .control
              }
          }
      }
      
      extension InteractionMode {
          /// Whether a one-finger drag moves the picture (or trackpad-style relative pointer) rather
          /// than sending absolute positions to the Mac.
          var oneFingerDragPansCanvas: Bool { self == .navigate || self == .observe || self == .trackpad }
      
          /// Whether the screen acts as a relative-delta trackpad (pointer follows the finger's
          /// movement, not its absolute position). `onPanCanvas` deltas are used instead of `onTouchMove`.
          var isTrackpad: Bool { self == .trackpad }
      
          /// Whether a two-finger drag moves the picture rather than scrolling the Mac.
          ///
          /// Always in drag & zoom. In view & scroll only while the picture is larger than the view
          /// it is drawn in, which is the one case where there is somewhere to pan *to*. Fitted, with
          /// bars down either side, the gesture keeps its other meaning and scrolls the Mac.
          ///
          /// **The rule is about the picture, not about history.** An earlier version keyed off the
          /// zoom alone, and was argued against on the grounds that the same gesture would do
          /// different things depending on what the user had done minutes earlier. That objection
          /// holds when the cause is invisible; it does not hold here, because a picture that
          /// overflows its view is the thing the user is looking at. The zoom is on screen in the
          /// pill besides — see the percentage readout in ``RemoteScreen``.
          func twoFingerDragPansCanvas(isZoomed: Bool) -> Bool {
              // Trackpad mode never pans the canvas — two fingers always scroll the Mac.
              if self == .trackpad { return false }
              return self != .control || isZoomed
          }
      
          /// Whether a tap is a click on the Mac. False in drag & zoom, where a tap is nothing at all
          /// — the mode exists so the picture can be moved and inspected without anything landing on
          /// the Mac by accident.
          var tapsReachTheMac: Bool { self == .control || self == .trackpad }
      
          /// Whether a pinch resizes the picture. Drag & zoom only.
          ///
          /// This was both modes, on the argument that looking closer is not a mode — that a user who
          /// wants to read a menu bar should not have to hand the pointer over to do it. The argument
          /// holds in principle and lost to the gesture beside it: in view & scroll the two fingers of
          /// a pinch *are* two fingers on the Mac, and a picture that grows under them is the one
          /// thing in that mode not supposed to move without being asked.
          ///
          /// Gating it costs nothing structurally, which is why the gate was left here to close. The
          /// guard below runs before `isPinching` is set, so a pinch in view & scroll never claims its
          /// fingers — and what it falls through to is the two-finger scroll, which is what those
          /// fingers were going to do anyway.
          var pinchZoomsCanvas: Bool { self != .control }
      }
      
      /// The gesture surface over the video.
      ///
      /// UIKit rather than SwiftUI gestures, for two reasons that are both about the pointer:
      ///
      /// - SwiftUI's `DragGesture` reports one touch and has no notion of *how many* fingers are
      ///   down, so "one finger drives the pointer, two fingers scroll" — the distinction every
      ///   remote desktop client makes — cannot be expressed with it at all.
      /// - A SwiftUI gesture's value is delivered through a view update, which means a cursor move
      ///   waits for a layout pass. On a 120 Hz display that is the difference between tracking the
      ///   finger and trailing it.
      struct CanvasGestureView: UIViewRepresentable {
          /// One finger, in the canvas's coordinate space.
          var onTouchDown: (CGPoint) -> Void
          var onTouchMove: (CGPoint) -> Void
          var onTouchUp: (CGPoint) -> Void
          /// Two-finger tap.
          var onSecondaryClick: (CGPoint) -> Void
          /// Two-finger drag: an incremental translation.
          var onScroll: (CGSize) -> Void
          /// Pinch: an incremental scale factor and the point it is centred on.
          var onZoom: (CGFloat, CGPoint) -> Void
          /// A drag that moves the picture instead of the Mac: one finger or two, in drag & zoom.
          var onPanCanvas: (CGSize) -> Void
          /// The exact bounds UIKit uses for gesture locations.
          var onSizeChange: (CGSize) -> Void
          /// Whether the fingers drive the Mac or the picture. See ``InteractionMode``.
          var interactionMode: InteractionMode
          /// Whether the picture is drawn larger than the view it is in — `RemoteScreen`'s
          /// `effectiveZoom > 1`. Read by the two-finger handler to decide whether a drag has
          /// anywhere to pan to, or should keep scrolling the Mac.
          var isZoomed: Bool = false
          /// The window's own chrome, in the canvas's coordinates. See ``CanvasGeometry/isBlocked``.
          var blockedRegions: [CGRect] = []
          /// Whether a floating panel is open. A tap on the picture then closes it instead of clicking
          /// the Mac — see ``GestureSurfaceView/handleTap(_:)``.
          var isPanelOpen: Bool = false
          /// Called when such a tap lands.
          var onDismissPanel: () -> Void = {}
          /// Whether the status bar is expanded. Same rule as a panel: a tap on the picture closes it
          /// rather than reaching the Mac. One tap does one thing.
          var isStatusBarExpanded: Bool = false
          var onCollapseStatusBar: () -> Void = {}
          /// Whether the software keyboard is up, and what to do when a tap lands on the picture.
          ///
          /// The same rule as a panel, for the same reason: the keyboard is a second thing of ours open
          /// over the picture, and a tap that misses it belongs to closing it.
          var isKeyboardOpen: Bool = false
          var onDismissKeyboard: () -> Void = {}
      
          func makeUIView(context: Context) -> GestureSurfaceView {
              let view = GestureSurfaceView()
              view.interactionMode = interactionMode
              view.isZoomed = isZoomed
              view.blockedRegions = blockedRegions
              view.isPanelOpen = isPanelOpen
              view.onDismissPanel = onDismissPanel
              view.isStatusBarExpanded = isStatusBarExpanded
              view.onCollapseStatusBar = onCollapseStatusBar
              view.isKeyboardOpen = isKeyboardOpen
              view.onDismissKeyboard = onDismissKeyboard
              view.handlers = .init(
                  onTouchDown: onTouchDown,
                  onTouchMove: onTouchMove,
                  onTouchUp: onTouchUp,
                  onSecondaryClick: onSecondaryClick,
                  onScroll: onScroll,
                  onZoom: onZoom,
                  onPanCanvas: onPanCanvas,
                  onSizeChange: onSizeChange
              )
              return view
          }
      
          func updateUIView(_ view: GestureSurfaceView, context: Context) {
              // The closures capture the current view state (zoom, pan, canvas size), so they are
              // replaced on every update rather than captured once in `makeUIView`.
              view.interactionMode = interactionMode
              view.isZoomed = isZoomed
              view.blockedRegions = blockedRegions
              view.isPanelOpen = isPanelOpen
              view.onDismissPanel = onDismissPanel
              view.isStatusBarExpanded = isStatusBarExpanded
              view.onCollapseStatusBar = onCollapseStatusBar
              view.isKeyboardOpen = isKeyboardOpen
              view.onDismissKeyboard = onDismissKeyboard
              view.handlers = .init(
                  onTouchDown: onTouchDown,
                  onTouchMove: onTouchMove,
                  onTouchUp: onTouchUp,
                  onSecondaryClick: onSecondaryClick,
                  onScroll: onScroll,
                  onZoom: onZoom,
                  onPanCanvas: onPanCanvas,
                  onSizeChange: onSizeChange
              )
          }
      }
      
      /// The view behind ``CanvasGestureView``.
      final class GestureSurfaceView: UIView, UIGestureRecognizerDelegate {
          struct Handlers {
              var onTouchDown: (CGPoint) -> Void
              var onTouchMove: (CGPoint) -> Void
              var onTouchUp: (CGPoint) -> Void
              var onSecondaryClick: (CGPoint) -> Void
              var onScroll: (CGSize) -> Void
              var onZoom: (CGFloat, CGPoint) -> Void
              var onPanCanvas: (CGSize) -> Void
              var onSizeChange: (CGSize) -> Void
          }
      
          var handlers: Handlers?
      
          /// Whether the fingers drive the Mac or the picture. Read by every handler below.

          /// button that opened it — which is a second target for a gesture the user has already made.
          var isPanelOpen = false
          var onDismissPanel: (() -> Void)?
          /// The status bar, by the same rule — see ``CanvasGestureView/isStatusBarExpanded``.
                                      .foregroundStyle(.white)
                                      .multilineTextAlignment(.center)
                              }
                              .padding(32)
                          }
                          // Hits pass through to the Mac, deliberately: an injected event is real
                          // user activity, and real user activity is what a sleeping display is

          /// axis of the picture matches the screen — which fills it, and crops the other axis, which
          /// is what the user then pans around in drag & zoom.
          private var fillZoom: CGFloat {
              guard canvasSize.width > 0, canvasSize.height > 0 else { return 1 }

                  onTouchDown: { point in

                      }
                      guard let normalized = normalized(point) else { return }
                      model.send(.mouseMove(to: normalized))
                      model.send(.mouseDown(button: .left))
                  },
                  onTouchMove: { point in
                      guard let normalized = normalized(point) else { return }
                      model.send(.mouseMove(to: normalized))
                  },
                  onTouchUp: { point in

                          model.send(.mouseUp(button: .left))

                      model.send(.mouseMove(to: normalized))
                      model.send(.mouseUp(button: .left))

                  },
                  onSecondaryClick: { point in
                      if interactionMode.isTrackpad {
                          model.send(.mouseDown(button: .right))

      /// The pointer's radius.

      /// A file-level constant because three numbers have to agree: the circle's size, the horizontal

      /// The remote desktop itself: picture, pointer, keyboard, and the controls for all three.

          /// it will want the next one filled too.

          private func presentLandscapeHint() {

          @objc private func handlePinch(_ gesture: UIPinchGestureRecognizer) {
              guard interactionMode.pinchZoomsCanvas else { return }
      
              // Set before the chrome check below, so a pinch that begins over a panel still owns its
              // two fingers and does not scroll the Mac behind the panel.
              switch gesture.state {
              case .began, .changed: isPinching = true
              case .ended, .cancelled, .failed: isPinching = false
              default: break
              }
      
              let point = gesture.location(in: self)
              guard !CanvasGeometry.isBlocked(point, regions: blockedRegions) else { return }
              let scale = gesture.scale
              gesture.scale = 1
              guard scale != 1 else { return }
              handlers?.onZoom(scale, point)
          }
      }
      
      /// Where the video is actually drawn inside the canvas.
      ///
      /// The layer letterboxes with `videoGravity = .resizeAspect`, so the picture occupies a
      /// centred rect whose aspect matches the *captured pixels*. Every touch has to be mapped
      /// through that rect, or the pointer lands proportionally wrong the moment the two aspects
      /// differ — which on a phone showing a 16:10 Mac display is always.
      enum CanvasGeometry {
          /// The layer's letterboxed rect for a captured size, inside `bounds`.
          static func videoRect(bounds: CGSize, pixelSize: CGSize?) -> CGRect {
              guard let pixelSize, pixelSize.width > 0, pixelSize.height > 0,
                    bounds.width > 0, bounds.height > 0
              else {
                  return CGRect(origin: .zero, size: bounds)
              }
              let scale = min(bounds.width / pixelSize.width, bounds.height / pixelSize.height)
              let size = CGSize(width: pixelSize.width * scale, height: pixelSize.height * scale)
              return CGRect(
                  x: (bounds.width - size.width) / 2,
                  y: (bounds.height - size.height) / 2,
                  width: size.width,
                  height: size.height
              )
          }
      
          /// Whether a touch belongs to the window's own chrome rather than to the Mac.
          ///
          /// The gesture surface is a UIKit view and the chrome is drawn over it by SwiftUI, which is
          /// not enough to keep a touch on a button from reaching the Mac as well. A pan recogniser
          /// begins after ten points of travel, and a thumb pressing a button near the edge of the
          /// screen slides further than that without meaning to — so the drag starts on the surface
          /// underneath and the Mac's pointer jumps to wherever the finger happened to be, while the
          /// button it was aimed at also fires. There is nothing in the geometry to tell the two apart;
          /// the caller knows where its chrome is, so it says so.
          static func isBlocked(_ point: CGPoint, regions: [CGRect]) -> Bool {
              regions.contains { $0.contains(point) }
          }
      
          /// Whether a tap should put something of ours away rather than reach the Mac.
          ///
          /// A panel and the software keyboard are the same thing from the picture's point of view:
          /// something of ours is open over it, and a tap on the picture belongs to closing that rather
          /// than to the Mac underneath. One decision asked twice with different answers for "is it

          /// panel out from under the tile the user was aiming at.
          static func dismissesOverlay(at point: CGPoint, regions: [CGRect], isOpen: Bool) -> Bool {
              isOpen && !isBlocked(point, regions: regions)

          /// switched. A client that sent pixels would be wrong the first time either happened.
          static func normalized(
              point: CGPoint,
              bounds: CGSize,
              pixelSize: CGSize?,

              pan: CGSize
          ) -> NormalizedPoint? {
              guard bounds.width > 0, bounds.height > 0, zoom > 0 else { return nil }
              let rect = videoRect(bounds: bounds, pixelSize: pixelSize)

              let centre = CGPoint(x: bounds.width / 2, y: bounds.height / 2)

          /// Whether a larger pointer is drawn into the video, and how much larger.
          ///

              super.layoutSubviews()
              let size = bounds.size
              guard size != lastReportedSize else { return }
              lastReportedSize = size
              // Deferring avoids mutating SwiftUI state in the middle of UIKit's layout pass.
              DispatchQueue.main.async { [weak self] in
                  self?.handlers?.onSizeChange(size)
              }
          }
      
          // MARK: - One finger: the pointer
      
          @objc private func handleOneFingerPan(_ gesture: UIPanGestureRecognizer) {
              let point = gesture.location(in: self)
      
              let isEnding = gesture.state == .ended
                  || gesture.state == .cancelled
                  || gesture.state == .failed
              if gesture.state == .began {
                  ignoresCurrentGesture = CanvasGeometry.isBlocked(point, regions: blockedRegions)
              }
              defer { if isEnding { ignoresCurrentGesture = false } }
              guard !ignoresCurrentGesture else { return }
      
              if interactionMode.oneFingerDragPansCanvas {
                  // Incremental, and the accumulator is reset each time, for the same reason the
                  // two-finger handler does it: a cumulative translation would make the view
                  // accelerate away from the finger.
                  let moved = gesture.translation(in: self)
                  gesture.setTranslation(.zero, in: self)
                  guard moved != .zero else { return }
                  handlers?.onPanCanvas(CGSize(width: moved.x, height: moved.y))
                  return
              }
      
              switch gesture.state {
              case .began:
                  // The press is posted *after* the move, and the ordering is load-bearing on the
                  // host: `mouseDown` is injected at the position of the last move it saw, so a
                  // click that overtook its own move would land wherever the cursor happened to be.
                  handlers?.onTouchDown(point)
              case .changed:
                  handlers?.onTouchMove(point)
              case .ended, .cancelled, .failed:
                  handlers?.onTouchUp(point)
              default:
                  break
              }
          }
      
          @objc private func handleTap(_ gesture: UITapGestureRecognizer) {
              let point = gesture.location(in: self)
              // Checked *before* the mode guard below, because a mode that makes taps inert on the Mac
              // is not a mode that makes them inert on this app's own chrome. A panel that could only
              // be dismissed in one of the two modes would be a bug the user has to discover.
              //
              // Blocked points are excluded so the panel's own buttons still work: a tap inside one
              // belongs to that button, and dismissing on it would close the panel out from under the
              // tile the user was aiming at.
              if CanvasGeometry.dismissesOverlay(at: point, regions: blockedRegions, isOpen: isPanelOpen) {
                  onDismissPanel?()
                  return
              }
              // After the panel, so a tap that closes a panel does not also put the keyboard away: one
              // tap, one thing closed, and the panel is the one on top.
              if CanvasGeometry.dismissesOverlay(at: point, regions: blockedRegions, isOpen: isKeyboardOpen) {
                  onDismissKeyboard?()
                  return
              }
              // The status bar, after the panels so that one tap closes one thing: if a panel is
              // open it is the thing on top, and it is the one the tap was aimed at.
              if CanvasGeometry.dismissesOverlay(at: point, regions: blockedRegions, isOpen: isStatusBarExpanded) {
                  onCollapseStatusBar?()
                  return
              }
              // Nothing at all in drag & zoom, which is the point of that mode: the picture can be
              // moved and inspected without a stray tap landing on whatever is under it.
              guard interactionMode.tapsReachTheMac else { return }
              guard !CanvasGeometry.isBlocked(point, regions: blockedRegions) else { return }
              handlers?.onTouchDown(point)
              handlers?.onTouchUp(point)
          }
      
          // MARK: - Two fingers
      
          @objc private func handleTwoFingerPan(_ gesture: UIPanGestureRecognizer) {
              // Two fingers that land on the chrome are scrolling a panel — the macro strip, the
              // desktop thumbnails — and must not also scroll the Mac behind it.
              if gesture.state == .began {
                  let start = gesture.location(in: self)
                  ignoresTwoFingerGesture = CanvasGeometry.isBlocked(start, regions: blockedRegions)
                  // Decided once, here, rather than per sample — see `twoFingerDragPansCanvas`.
                  twoFingerDragPansCanvas = interactionMode.twoFingerDragPansCanvas(isZoomed: isZoomed)
              }
              if gesture.state == .ended || gesture.state == .cancelled || gesture.state == .failed {
                  ignoresTwoFingerGesture = false
              }
              guard !ignoresTwoFingerGesture else { return }
      
              // The pinch owns both fingers while it lasts. Its translation is the user repositioning
              // the picture under the pinch, not a scroll wheel.
              guard !isPinching else { return }
      
              let point = gesture.translation(in: self)
              // Incremental, and the recogniser's accumulator is reset each time so the next
              // callback reports only what moved since. Re-sending the cumulative translation would
              // make the scroll accelerate away under the user's fingers.
              gesture.setTranslation(.zero, in: self)
      
              guard point != .zero else { return }
              // A translation is a displacement, not a position: both axes are signed and neither
              // has an origin, which is what `CGSize` means here and `CGPoint` would misdescribe.
              let translation = CGSize(width: point.x, height: point.y)
              if twoFingerDragPansCanvas {
                  handlers?.onPanCanvas(translation)
              } else {
                  handlers?.onScroll(translation)
              }
          }
      
          @objc private func handleTwoFingerTap(_ gesture: UITapGestureRecognizer) {
              // Same rule as the one-finger tap: a right click is still a click.
              guard interactionMode.tapsReachTheMac else { return }
              let point = gesture.location(in: self)
              guard !CanvasGeometry.isBlocked(point, regions: blockedRegions) else { return }
              handlers?.onSecondaryClick(point)
